II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
5 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel’s                
6 
testimony? 
7 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to provide a forecast of the capital 
8 
investments of the Company for the twelve-month period ending March 31, 
9 
2021 (“Rate Year”), and the three subsequent twelve-month periods ending 
10 
March 31, 2022 (“Data Year 1”), March 31, 2023 (“Data Year 2”), and March 
11 
31, 2024 (“Data Year 3”) (Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3 are 
12 
collectively referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel also presents a forecast 
13 
for the fifteen-month period (“Gap Period”) between December 31, 2018, 
14 
which is the end of the Company’s Historic Test Year, and the beginning of the 
15 
Rate Year.    
16 
 
17 
The Panel discusses direct capital expenditures that will (i) increase the safety 
18 
and reliability of the Company’s gas network, (ii) modernize the Company’s 
19 
gas transmission and distribution infrastructure to reduce emissions while 
20 
continuing to meet customer needs, and (iii) enhance resiliency and the 
21 


--- Page 9 ---
 
 
Page 7 of 120 
Company’s ability to respond to future weather events.  The Panel will also 
1 
discuss the Company’s practices and policies for maximizing the efficiency of 
2 
its capital delivery program from planning and budgeting through the 
3 
completion of construction.    
4 
  
5 
 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of the significant projects in the 
6 
Company’s capital plan, including retirement of leak prone pipe (“LPP”) and 
7 
new safety programs to identify and address system risks.  The Panel’s 
8 
testimony also presents an overview of the Company’s pipeline integrity and 
9 
reliability programs that will improve the overall safety and reliability of the 
10 
Company’s gas system, and will also address recently enacted, as well as 
11 
pending, pipeline safety regulations administered by the U.S. Department of 
12 
Transportation 
(“DOT”), 
Pipeline 
and 
Hazardous 
Materials 
Safety 
13 
Administration (“PHMSA”).  The Panel discusses the Company’s plans and 
14 
infrastructure projects necessary to ensure that the Company can reliably serve 
15 
its existing customers as well as forecast demand for new customer connections 
16 
within the Company’s operating territory. 
17 
 
 
18 
 
The Panel also presents the Company’s indirect capital investments, including 
19 
supply chain, inventory management/investment recovery (“IM/IR”), and fleet 
20 
investments.  The Company is also proposing significant investments in 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
 
 
Page 8 of 120 
facilities during the Rate Year and Data Years that are necessary to: support the 
1 
Company’s O&M and gas safety programs; make needed upgrades to aging 
2 
facilities; and create more modern and efficient customer support and employee 
3 
work spaces.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s testimony also address the Company’s operations and 
6 
maintenance (“O&M”) programs? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to capital investments in gas infrastructure, the Panel describes 
8 
incremental labor (full time equivalent positions or “FTEs”) and non-labor 
9 
O&M expenses that the Company proposes in the Rate Year and Data Years, 
10 
the costs of which are not fully reflected in the twelve-month period beginning 
11 
January 1, 2018 and ending December 31, 2018 (“Historic Test Year”).  These 
12 
expenses represent known and measurable changes from Historic Test Year 
13 
expenses that are necessary to (i) improve system safety and reliability, (ii) 
14 
address new and emerging safety regulations, (iii) enhance customer service, 
15 
and (iv) support the Company’s capital investments.  The Panel will also discuss 
16 
the Company’s staffing plan for the proposed new FTEs. 
17 
 
 
18

17

18

19

20

Page 11 of 120 
mains and services, and other key capital investments in gas pipeline safety 
1 
and reliability.   
2 
Section IV provides details on the Company’s proposed direct capital 
3

4

5 
activities within each category.    
6

7

18

Page 12 of 120 
III. 
Capital and O&M Plan Objectives and Priorities 
1 
A. Plan Objectives 
2 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s overall objective of its infrastructure and 
3 
operations programs. 
4 
A. 
The Company designs its gas infrastructure and operations programs to provide 
5 
safe and reliable gas delivery service to customers at reasonable costs.  Over 
6 
the last several years, the Company has significantly increased investment to 
7 
modernize and enhance the resiliency of its gas assets.  Significant capital 
8 
investment over the next several years is required to ensure that the gas system 
9 
continues to operate safely while meeting the demands of customers and to 
10 
enable further modernization of the Company’s facilities and operations in a 
11 
manner that achieves meaningful methane emissions reductions in furtherance 
12 
of the Company’s Future of Heat strategy.  The proposed plan includes capital 
13 
and O&M spending to meet both customer needs and satisfy state and federal 
14 
regulatory requirements and goals, including retirement of LPP, gas pipeline 
15 
safety enhancements, and deployment of non-pipeline alternatives.  While 
16 
significant investments are required to meet these needs and objectives, in 
17 
developing its capital and O&M plans, the Company balanced the need for 
18 
spending to achieve safety and service objectives with the need to manage costs 
19 
and minimize impacts on customer rates.     
20 
 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
 
 
Page 13 of 120

What are the Company’s projected direct and indirect capital forecasts?

A. 
A summary of the Company’s total capital plan forecast for the Rate Year and 
2 
Data Years is provided in Table 1. 
3

7 
Q. 
Why is the scale of the Company’s capital investment plan increasing? 
8 
A. 
Several developments have required that KEDNY and other natural gas 
9 
distribution utilities increase their annual capital expenditures.  Notably, 
10 
pipeline safety incidents, such as the tragic events in San Bruno, California, 
11 
Allentown, Pennsylvania, East Harlem, New York, and the more recent incident 
12 
in Merrimack Valley, Massachusetts, have appropriately increased focus on 
13 
pipeline safety and the need to carefully monitor and replace aging pipeline 
14 
infrastructure.  Weather events, such as Superstorm Sandy and the Polar Vortex, 
15 
and the expectation that similar events will continue to occur, require the 
16 
Company to find ways to protect its facilities from severe weather.   
17 
 
18 


--- Page 16 ---
 
 
Page 14 of 120 
In response to these events, the industry has adopted new safety requirements 
1 
and is implementing best practices to modernize and protect gas facilities.  For 
2 
example, since the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, (i) new Commission 
3 
safety regulations and directives now require additional safety inspections of 
4 
inside service lines and plastic fusions; (ii) PHMSA is implementing new safety 
5 
requirements related to transmission system integrity management and 
6 
verification this year; and (iii) the New York State Legislature is currently 
7 
considering a bill that would require licensed professional engineers (“PEs”) to 
8 
review and certify gas facility design plans.  The Commission and utility 
9 
industry review of recent incidents has also resulted in recommendations for 
10 
safety best practices, including overpressure protection improvements and 
11 
enhanced contractor oversight.  These mandated and recommended 
12 
improvements require significant additional investments and compliance 
13 
resources. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How will the Company support the increased level of capital investment? 
16 
A. 
As the Company developed plans to modernize its gas assets, it also began to 
17 
build and enhance its operations, engineering, resource planning, work 
18 
management, and quality control organizations and capabilities to deliver 
19 
increasing levels of capital investment.  The Company will continue to develop 
20 
these capabilities in the Rate Year and Data Years by adding incremental labor 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
 
 
Page 15 of 120 
resources to execute the capital plan and support the increased operations 
1 
workload (discussed in Section VI).  The capital investment plan includes 
2 
additional field training resources, facilities, and tools to enhance the 
3 
development of the internal work force.  
4 
 
5 
Regarding contractor resources, over the past several years, the Company 
6 
developed a procurement strategy that supports sustainable growth in qualified 
7 
contractors to meet the work plan increases.  To ensure adequate levels of 
8 
qualified, skilled labor, and to address the challenges around developing 
9 
qualified contractors, the Company has worked to:  
10 
• Establish longer term contracts to enable contractors to plan and invest 
11 
in hiring, training, facilities, and equipment to meet the Company’s 
12 
construction needs  
13 
• Provide greater work plan visibility to contractors on forecast crew 
14 
requirements, which will enable them to develop the required capacity 
15 
• Manage the work plan to limit seasonal variability to support a stable 
16 
contractor workforce and promote worker retention 
17 
• Continue partnerships with community colleges, trade schools, and 
18 
veterans group to attract and develop new sources of skilled labor to 
19 
build the workforce  
20 
 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
 
 
Page 16 of 120 
Q. 
Does the Company require additional personnel in the Rate Year and Data 
1 
Years to execute its capital and O&M programs? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Company forecasts the need for an additional 346.7 FTE positions by 
3 
Data Year 3 to support the additional capital investment, increasing O&M 
4 
workload due to new and pending regulatory requirements, new programs 
5 
discussed below, and the gas safety programs presented in the Gas Safety 
6 
Panel’s testimony.  These FTEs include positions in contractor oversight, gas 
7 
pipeline 
safety 
management, 
corrosion 
control, 
field 
operations, 
8 
instrumentation and regulation (“I&R”), gas control, gas system engineering, 
9 
and gas system management.  The cost of these FTEs will be charged to both 
10 
capital and O&M programs based on the job function and nature of the work.  
11 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-9) identifies the incremental FTE positions by function and 
12 
the need for each position.  KEDNY’s Revenue Requirements Panel presents 
13 
the labor O&M associated with these FTEs.   
14 
 
15 
B. Cost Pressures and Cost Mitigation 
16 
Q. 
What explains the significant increases reflected in the investment plan?  
17 
A. 
The Company’s investment plan reflects increases in both the scope of work 
18 
(units) and the costs elements to perform this work (unit costs).  As discussed 
19 
above, there are several factors that are collectively increasing the size of the 
20 
Company’s investment plan over the next several years, including incremental 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
 
 
Page 17 of 120 
work driven by federal and state regulatory mandates and the need to upgrade 
1 
aged infrastructure and replace dated facilities.  At the same time, the Company 
2 
is experiencing increasing unit costs resulting from new work requirements and 
3 
enhanced safety practices, and the rising costs of materials and contractors, in 
4 
part due to increasing demands for these resources across the industry  
5 
 
6 
Indeed, a primary driver of increases in the capital plan is significantly higher 
7 
unit costs for main installation work across multiple mandated programs, 
8 
including the proactive and reactive main replacement, customer connections, 
9 
corrosion, and city/state construction (“CSC”) programs.  The unit cost   
10 
increases are explained in more detail, below, and unit cost component 
11 
increases for main replacement are shown on Exhibit __ (GIOP-2).   
12 
 
13 
Another driver of the capital investment plan is a significant increase in the 
14 
volume of work in the Company’s CSC program, which involves the relocation 
15 
and/or protection of gas facilities that conflict with municipal public works 
16 
projects.  As described in detail below, the CSC program scope is continuing to 
17 
grow as New York City is investing billions of dollars to improve its water and 
18 
sewer systems.  As a result of the sharp increase in scope and scale of the City’s 
19 
public works programs, the CSC program now represents more than a third of 
20 


--- Page 20 ---
 
 
Page 18 of 120 
the Company’s total capital investment plan, and the work requirements and 
1 
timing of this program are not within the Company’s control.         
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What factors are contributing to the Company’s unit cost increases?   
4 
A. 
Exhibit __ (GIOP- 2) shows the components that have contributed to the main 
5 
installation unit cost increase.  The primary source of increased costs is New 
6 
York City’s new restoration and paving requirements that became effective in 
7 
April 2017.  Specifically, changes to Highway Rules §§ 2-01 through 2-09, 2-
8 
11 through 2-14, and 2-20 (“Paving Rules”) now require significantly more 
9 
restoration work, including: 
10 
• Increasing the size of the area that must be re-paved following an 
11 
excavation.  Under the new Paving Rules, the final restoration area is based 
12 
on the largest dimension of the excavation.  Under the prior regulations, 
13 
KEDNY was authorized to pave the area of individual bell holes.  The 
14 
Paving Rules require KEDNY to pave the bell holes in addition to the area 
15 
in between those holes.  In some cases, this requirement doubles or triples 
16 
the area needing to be repaved.   
17 
• Requiring that base material be replaced in kind or better, which means that 
18 
concrete roadways must be replaced with concrete instead of the less 
19 
expensive binder material that was previously utilized.  In that regard, 
20 
approximately 65 percent of New York City streets are concrete; and   
21 


--- Page 21 ---
 
 
Page 19 of 120 
• Requiring more saw cutting of roadway excavations instead of more 
1 
efficient and cost-effective jackhammering.   
2 
The Historic Test Year was the first full year in which these new paving costs 
3 
were effective, during which the Company performed tens of thousands of 
4 
individual paving jobs in the City at a considerably increased cost.  Indeed, in 
5 
calendar year (“CY”) 2017 to CY 2018, paving restoration costs accounted for 
6 
approximately 46 percent ($526) of the total main replacement unit cost 
7 
($1,147).   
8 
 
9 
 
Other factors contributing to an increase in the Company’s main replacement 
10 
unit cost, as shown on Exhibit __ (GIOP-2), include: 
11 
• Higher base labor (contractor costs): recently renegotiated long-term 
12 
contractor agreements resulted in higher base labor contractor costs  
13 
• Mains Size: work scope includes more large-diameter main at higher 
14 
replacement costs 
15 
• Increased time and materials (“T&M”): increasingly restrictive 
16 
municipal stipulations that limit work time in streets and increasing 
17 
costs to comply with arborist regulations (protection of trees on 
18 
sidewalks proximate to work sites)  
19

i. 
Proactive Main Replacement (LPP) Program 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the inventory of LPP existing on the Company’s system.   
13 
A. 
LPP in KEDNY’s service territory is comprised of twelve-inch and smaller 
14 
main and associated services that are (i) unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically 
15 
protected) steel pipe whether bare or coated and (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, 
16 
(iii) unprotected steel/wrought iron services, and (iv) copper and pre-1974 
17 
HDPE services.  As of the end of CY 2018, the Company has approximately 
18 
1,458 miles of LPP in its remaining inventory. 
19 
 
 
20 


--- Page 29 ---
 
 
Page 27 of 120 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposal regarding retirement of LPP? 
1 
A. 
To reduce the risk of leaks and breaks, improve system safety, performance, 
2 
and reliability, meet the Company’s commitment to enhance customer 
3 
satisfaction, and reduce methane emissions, the Company has prioritized the 
4 
retirement of poor performing and higher-risk gas infrastructure – specifically, 
5 
LPP and associated services – that disproportionally contributes to leaks on 
6 
KEDNY’s system.  The Company’s current rate plan funds LPP replacements 
7 
of 55 miles in CY 2017, 60 miles in CY 2018, and 65 miles in CY 2019.   
8 
 
9 
The Company’s proposal is to continue accelerating LPP retirement by five 
10 
miles per year from the current, CY 2019 target of 65 miles, as depicted in Table 
11 
2.   The performance metrics for LPP replacement, including minimum mileage 
12 
targets, are set forth in more detail in the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel 
13 
testimony.  
14

15

16 
The proposed LPP retirement rate will position the Company to fully retire its 
17

--- Page 30 ---
 
 
Page 28 of 120 
 
1 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing to continue accelerating LPP retirement 
2 
by five additional miles per year? 
3 
A. 
As would be expected, the Company has observed a significantly higher leak 
4 
rate on its LPP inventory as compared to all other distribution facilities.  While 
5 
the current LPP inventory represents only 35 percent of KEDNY’s distribution 
6 
system, LPP is responsible for 96 percent of leak repairs, excluding excavation 
7 
damages.  The current leak rate for all distribution piping is 0.81 leaks per mile.  
8 
The 2018 leak rate for LPP was 2.23 leaks per mile, representing an increase 
9 
from the 2017 leak rate for LPP of 1.97.    
10 
 
11 
Significantly, in KEDNY’s service territory, the leak rate for LPP has continued 
12 
to increase over the past several years, as shown in Table 3.  In fact, the 2018 
13 
LPP leak rate was the highest in the past ten years.  Thus, KEDNY’s proposal 
14 
to continue accelerating LPP retirement is warranted.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-4) 
15 
provides projected leak rates for LPP for various main replacement strategies. 
16 
 
 
17

1 
 
2

6

7

8

9

Page 31 of 120 
Q. 
How does the Company prioritize the retirement of main segments for the 
1 
Proactive Main Replacement Program? 
2 
A. 
Each year, the Company prioritizes retirement of LPP segments by using a risk-
3 
ranking algorithm that is part of the Company’s Distribution Integrity 
4 
Management Plan (“DIMP”) and the Company’s Gas Operating Procedure for 
5 
the Identification, Evaluation and Prioritization of Distribution Main Segments 
6 
for Replacement (ENG04030).  The Company’s risk model calculates a relative 
7 
risk score for each LPP segment based on specific performance data and 
8 
localized incident probabilities and consequences, combined with calculated 
9 
risk factors for the asset classes under evaluation.  This risk-based algorithm, 
10 
along with the Company’s engineering judgment, which takes all factors and 
11 
risks into consideration in each case, form the foundation of the LPP retirement 
12 
strategy.   
13 
 
14 
The Company is proposing to target more cast iron main for retirement where 
15 
possible in consideration of other risk factors, due to increased age and risk of 
16 
this material.  Additionally, as a result of the Storm Hardening Collaborative 
17 
following the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Company added flood 
18 
zone factors to its risk ranking algorithm such that, where other risk factors are 
19 
equal, the Company will target LPP retirements in Federal Emergency 
20 
Management Agency (“FEMA”) 100 and 500-year flood zones.   
21 


--- Page 34 ---

7 
ii. 
Gas Safety Integrity Management and Integrity Verification:  IMP 
8 
and IVP Programs 
9 
Q. 
What is the Company’s IMP? 
10 
A. 
The Company’s transmission pipeline IMP is a safety program mandated by the 
11 
Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 and corresponding DOT regulations.  
12 
The IMP identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the physical 
13 
soundness of Company facilities before they become safety or performance 
14 
issues.  The Company conducts baseline and periodic reassessments of 
15 
transmission facilities to identify and evaluate potential threats to “Covered 
16 
Segments” of pipelines, i.e., transmission pipelines that could affect High 
17 
Consequence Areas (areas where a pipeline failure could have significant 
18 
adverse consequences), as well as remediation of significant defects discovered 
19 
during such assessments.  In regions of the United States where older gas 
20 


--- Page 37 ---
 
 
Page 35 of 120 
distribution systems are common, IMPs have become a key component of gas 
1 
pipeline safety through improving pipeline integrity.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investments.  
4 
A. 
Currently, IMP assessments may be accomplished through either external 
5 
corrosion direct assessment (“ECDA”) or in-line inspection (“ILI”) technology, 
6 
which uses a robotic device known as a “pig” to evaluate the condition of the 
7 
inside of the pipeline.  ILI is considered superior to ECDA because of the ability 
8 
to identify condition problems originating internally.  IMP investments are 
9 
necessary to: (i) support in-line inspections (e.g., installation of pig launchers 
10 
and receivers, and pipe reconfiguration/replacement), and (ii) to resolve issues 
11 
discovered during pipeline inspections conducted through either method.   
12 
 
13 
The construction activities associated with these expenditures involve the 
14 
installation of “hot tap” fittings, the reconfiguration of such fittings to allow ILI 
15 
passage, the construction of access points to allow tethered in-line inspection 
16 
and, in some cases, the replacement of pipeline segments. 
17 
 
 
18 


--- Page 38 ---
 
 
Page 36 of 120 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investment needs for the Rate Year and 
1 
Data Years. 
2 
Currently, 21 percent of the Company’s DOT pipeline is ILI enabled, increased 
3 
from 17 percent when the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases was filed.  
4 
Over the long term, the Company’s plan is to reach 80 percent ILI enablement 
5 
of the Company’s DOT piping.  The Company’s IMP capital investments in the 
6 
Rate Year and Data Years will drive toward this goal and will significantly 
7 
improve the Company’s ability to identify integrity issues.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
How are the IMP workplan and associated IMP capital investment forecast 
10 
determined?  
11 
A. 
The workplan began in 2002 based on initial assessments using the Company’s 
12 
existing risk model.  Segments were prioritized for ILI enablement over a multi-
13 
year workplan based on a combination of their risk score and other relevant 
14 
factors, such as facility characteristics or geography, to determine what work to 
15 
do when.  Some segments must be re-assessed in certain years according to 
16 
when they were initially assessed.  The workplan is updated annually and as 
17 
required assessments are completed.  Additionally, ILI projects typically 
18 
consist of two years of design and procurement work followed by construction 
19 
in the third year.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 39 ---
 
 
Page 37 of 120 
IMP spending can fluctuate significantly year to year depending on the projects 
1 
identified for inclusion in the workplan.  Because the work must be done by 
2 
segment, the length, geographic location, and characteristics of the segments 
3 
that need to be addressed each year contribute to different costs such that 
4 
historic program costs are not indicative of future program needs.  Once 
5 
segments to be addressed by the workplan are identified, cost forecasts are level 
6 
one project estimates based on prior projects of similar size and scope adjusted 
7 
for known variables.  Additionally, some larger projects in the IMP are 
8 
separately budgeted on the capital investment plan.       
9 
 
10 
The IMP workplan for the Rate Year and Data Years is described in more detail 
11 
in the IMP data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The IMP capital investments 
12 
required to address known risks identified via assessments and to increase ILI 
13 
enablement are approximately $5.5 million in the Rate Year, $21.5 million in 
14 
Data Year 1, $20.0 million in Data Year 2, and $28.0 million in Data Year 3.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What is the status of the pending federal regulations in this area?   
17 
A. 
In May 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (“NPRM”) that 
18 
proposes new pipeline safety regulations that include a requirement for 
19 
increased inspection of IMP-covered pipelines utilizing ILI technology and an 
20 
expanded definition of High Consequence Areas, as well as a host of other 
21 


--- Page 40 ---
 
 
Page 38 of 120 
requirements.  To meet these requirements, transmission pipelines must be ILI 
1 
enabled.  
2 
  
3 
The Company expects that regulations will become effective in the fall of CY 
4 
2019, and an implementation schedule will be included.  Because the 
5 
Company’s IMP workplan is risk based, and in light of PHMSA’s new 
6 
regulations expanding IMP, the Company believes that its proposed IMP 
7 
program is a reasonable and conservative approach to managing pipeline 
8 
integrity during the Rate Year and Data Years.    
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What is covered in the Company’s IVP Program? 
11 
A. 
The Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 also mandates that PHMSA establish rules 
12 
requiring operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “fit for service” by 
13 
reviewing construction records for each pipeline segment to confirm it is 
14 
operating within design parameters.  The May 2016 NPRM also proposes new 
15 
rules regarding the maximum allowable operating pressure (“MAOP”) and 
16 
pressure testing requirements for existing pipelines, including (i) eliminating 
17 
the exemption for establishing the MAOP of pre-1970 “grandfathered” pipe 
18 
segments; (ii) mandating additional pressure testing or replacement for 
19 
pipelines without adequate pressure test records; and (iii) requiring operators 
20 
who lack certain records to establish material properties using approved 
21 


--- Page 41 ---
 
 
Page 39 of 120 
methods (e.g., cutting and testing pipe samples).  In advance of a final 
1 
rulemaking, PHMSA issued an advisory bulletin (ADB-11-01) directing 
2 
operators to perform a detailed threat and risk analysis that includes a records 
3 
review of their systems.   
4 
 
5 
The Company’s IVP program began in 2011 and includes thorough record 
6 
reviews, pipeline replacement, and retirement of non-essential pipeline 
7 
segments.  As with the IMP, the IVP is primarily based on the Company’s 
8 
assessment of system risks, while also incorporating PHMSA’s proposed 
9 
rulemaking.   
10 
 
11 
Q 
What is the status of the Company’s IVP records review and its IVP 
12 
Program proposal for the Rate Year and Data Years? 
13 
A. 
Through its IVP to date, KEDNY has completed the MAOP records review on 
14 
100 percent of its DOT-jurisdictional pipelines.  Going forward, the IVP 
15 
addresses transmission main replacements and testing necessitated by 
16 
incomplete records identified by the review and pressure testing and preparing 
17 
to comply with additional testing that will be required by the new rules.  Where 
18 
the Company has identified incomplete records, pipelines will be replaced, or 
19 
records will be recreated through testing.  This work is necessary to ensure 
20 
system integrity regardless of PHMSA’s proposed requirements.  The IVP work 
21 


--- Page 42 ---
 
 
Page 40 of 120 
plan is described in more detail in the IVP data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5) 
1 
and is levelized by year to manage spending at between approximately $4 and 
2 
$6 million in the Rate Year and each subsequent year. 
3 
 
4 
iii. 
Transmission Station Integrity Program 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Station Integrity Program. 
6 
A. 
This program is similar to the Company’s IVP, but the program addresses 
7 
transmission-connected pressure regulating stations and take stations 
8 
(“transmission stations”).  PHMSA’s 2016 NPRM, related advisory bulletins, 
9 
and discussions between PHMSA and industry stakeholders during the NPRM 
10 
process, have indicated that the proposed rules will apply to transmission 
11 
stations.  Therefore, the Company is proposing a program for IVP records 
12 
review and, where records are inadequate to demonstrate that transmission 
13 
station facilities are fit for service, capital investments for station rehabilitation 
14 
or partial or full station replacements.  Similar to the IMP and IVP Programs 
15 
for transmission pipelines, these investments are prudent in advance of the final 
16 
rulemaking to address risk presented by older pressure regulating facilities for 
17 
which there are inadequate records.  The Company is beginning to ramp-up this 
18 
program in FY 2020, and reaches full implementation in Data Year 1, and the 
19 
spending is expected to levalize year to year after Data Year 1.  The forecast for 
20 
this program is based on the number and vintage of transmission stations the 
21 


--- Page 43 ---
 
 
Page 41 of 120 
program is expected to address and level one estimates of similar station project 
1 
rehabilitation and replacement projects historically.   The capital investments 
2 
required for this program are $3 million in the Rate Year and over $17 million 
3 
in each of the Data Years.   
4 
     
5 
iv. 
CSC 
6 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the CSC Program. 
7 
A. 
The CSC Program is a mandated program that represents 34 percent of 
8 
KEDNY’s direct capital investment plan in the Rate Year.  This program 
9 
consists of construction work performed to accommodate third-party, 
10 
municipal construction activity that could impact the integrity of the 
11 
Company’s natural gas facilities.  Typical third-party construction activities that 
12 
impact gas facilities include work on water, sewer, and drainage infrastructure, 
13 
street reconstruction, road realignment, and bridge replacement.  State 
14 
regulations and Company procedures require the replacement of eight inch and 
15 
smaller cast iron gas mains if roadway or underground construction is being 
16 
performed in such a way that would impact the integrity of the Company’s 
17 
mains.  Non-cast iron gas mains (i.e., steel and plastic) are not subject to the 
18 
same replacement regulations and are typically supported and protected if not 
19 
in direct conflict with third-party construction.  Direct conflicts are addressed 
20 
through relocation regardless of material type.   
21 


--- Page 44 ---
 
 
Page 42 of 120 
 
1 
Aging municipal infrastructure (buildings, schools, bridges, roadways, 
2 
transportation systems, water mains and sewer facilities) will require significant 
3 
upgrades in the coming years.  Over the last several years, there has been an 
4 
increase in the level of municipal construction activity, as Superstorm Sandy 
5 
and recent gas incidents focused attention on the state of municipal 
6 
infrastructure.  Going forward, New York City will invest billions of dollars to 
7 
upgrade its infrastructure, and many of these projects will directly impact 
8 
KEDNY’s gas system.  Thus, the CSC Program is one of the largest programs 
9 
in the mandated category in terms of costs.  The forecast cost for this program 
10 
is approximately $324 million in the Rate Year, comprised of reimbursable, 
11 
non-reimbursable, and a large project that is separately budgeted and described 
12 
in more detail below.  
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Are there opportunities to retire LPP during CSC projects? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  As part of the CSC construction program, the Company looks to identify 
16 
cost-effective opportunities to retire LPP when main replacements are required 
17 
to accommodate municipal construction.  CSC projects present opportunities to 
18 
perform safety and reliability upgrades on the Company’s infrastructure, the 
19 
costs of which can be offset by coordinating construction activities (shared 
20 
trenching and paving) and securing third party reimbursements.  Of the 
21 


--- Page 45 ---
 
 
Page 43 of 120 
approximately 381,686 linear feet (72.3 miles) of CSC main replacements 
1 
forecast for FY 2020, approximately 163,680 linear feet (31 miles) of LPP is 
2 
expected to be retired.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How does the Company forecast CSC expenditures? 
 
5 
A. 
KEDNY forecasts its CSC expenditures by reviewing the known and planned 
6 
work identified by municipalities, historical work volumes, and unit 
7 
information.  In large part, the forecast is based on New York State and New 
8 
York City’s current five-year construction plans.  In CY 2018, approximately 
9 
317,948 linear feet (approximately 60.2 miles) of main replacement was 
10 
required to address municipal infrastructure improvements.  Thirty percent of 
11 
this footage for this replacement work (equating to approximately $108 million) 
12 
was subject to some level of reimbursement from third parties, while the 
13 
remaining 70 percent of the footage (approximately $156 million) was non-
14 
reimbursable main and service replacements.  Large, complex construction 
15 
projects, such as the SE856 Phase 2 Transmission Offset Project at Sheffield 
16 
and New Jersey Avenue (“SE856 Project”), described in more detail below, 
17 
may be project managed by the Company’s Capital Delivery organization and 
18 
are budgeted separately from the CSC Blanket Program.   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 46 ---
 
 
Page 44 of 120 
While the Company’s CSC forecasts are reasonable based on available 
1 
information, capital and O&M expenditures in this area are subject to a high 
2 
degree of variability because the scope and scheduling of municipal 
3 
construction projects are constantly revised.  The New York City Department 
4 
of Design and Construction’s (“DDC”) work plan is dynamic and is subject to 
5 
change even within a fiscal year (“FY”).  Because the Company does not 
6 
control the scope or timing of projects, large walk-in projects can require the 
7 
Company to undertake substantial unplanned capital work in any given year.  
8 
For example, in CY 2018, the Flatlands Transmission Offset project was added 
9 
to the DDC’s workplan.  This was not a known project that was included in the 
10 
Company’s CSC program during the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  
11 
The project was subsequently added to the FY 2020 work plan at an estimated 
12 
cost of approximately $69.4 million.  The project is expected to be complete 
13 
prior to the Rate Year.    
14 
 
15 
There is similar volatility with respect to CSC Program O&M expenditures, 
16 
some of which are non-reimbursable “Support and Protect” expenditures.  As 
17 
shown in Exhibit __ (GIOP- 6), the Company’s actual spending on non-
18 
reimbursable O&M varied from expected O&M expenditures based on DDC’s 
19 
work portfolio by 75 percent between FY 2016 and FY 2017, and by 118 
20 
percent between FY 2017 and FY 2018.  The dramatic year-to-year fluctuation 
21 


--- Page 47 ---
 
 
Page 45 of 120 
of the CSC Program’s O&M requirements are a significant impediment to 
1 
creation of a reliable multiyear financial O&M forecast.  
2 
    
3 
Q.  
Does the Company’s forecast reflect potential reimbursement from 
4 
municipalities and other third parties? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  KEDNY’s forecast reflects reimbursable amounts for expenditures 
6 
pursuant to the Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act and other cost sharing 
7 
arrangements with municipalities.  KEDNY forecasts expected reimbursements 
8 
total approximately $57.8 million in the Rate Year, $60.8 million in Data Year 
9 
1, $63.1 million in Data Year 2, and $64.9 million in Data Year 3.  However, 
10 
typically there is a one-and-a-half-year lag in payment of invoices by the City.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe New York City’s joint bidding process and its potential 
13 
impact on CSC costs. 
14 
A. 
In 2014, New York State adopted a “joint bidding” requirement for public 
15 
works projects in cities with populations of one million or more (N.Y.S. 
16 
Assembly Bill A10021B) that will impact KEDNY’s CSC projects in New 
17 
York City.  The City has not yet fully implemented this process.  Currently, 
18 
utilities impacted by New York City public works projects are individually 
19 
responsible for performing their own relocation work, as well as negotiating all 
20 
support and protect work directly with the City’s contractor.  Under the “joint 
21 


--- Page 48 ---
 
 
Page 46 of 120 
bidding” model, the City will bid and manage the entire project.  If the City’s 
1 
contractor is qualified by the Company to work on its gas facilities, the 
2 
contractor may perform the gas relocation work.  If the City’s contractor is not 
3 
gas qualified, KEDNY will perform its own relocation work ahead of the 
4 
planned project.  Each impacted utility will also be responsible for a portion of 
5 
the “shared costs” of work performed by the City’s contractors, including, for 
6 
example, maintaining the construction site, establishing field offices, setting up 
7 
transportation and managing contracts and expenses.  It is unclear when the City 
8 
will fully implement the joint bidding process, but in recent months, the City 
9 
has increased discussions regarding implementation.  It is likely that the process 
10 
will be fully implemented within the next few years, but the scope of its 
11 
application is currently unknown.  
12 
 
13 
The City’s workplan for FY 2019 and FY 2020 includes approximately 18 
14 
projects, across multiple boroughs and utilities, using the joint bidding process.  
15 
Incremental costs of the joint bidding process for FY 2019 and FY 2020 appear 
16 
to be about $0.5 million for each FY; however, the projects the City is jointly 
17 
bidding are not necessarily representative of future project scopes that may use 
18 
this process.  Because the true cost implications on utilities of working through 
19 
the City’s selected contractors, as well as the impact of the shared costs 
20 


--- Page 49 ---
 
 
Page 47 of 120 
contribution, remains to be seen, the Company has not included additional costs 
1 
in its CSC forecast.     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How does KEDNY address increasing costs in the CSC workplan and 
4 
reimbursements?       
5 
A. 
KEDNY works closely with DDC’s engineers, consultants, and contractors to 
6 
minimize direct conflicts to the Company’s existing facilities to reduce support 
7 
and protect and replacement work as much as practicable and to address 
8 
changes to DDC’s workplan.  Regarding invoicing and payments, KEDNY 
9 
recently adopted process improvements to improve the timeliness of Company 
10 
invoices to the City and is actively negotiating with the City regarding the 
11 
payment backlog and other process issues related to administration of the CSC 
12 
program.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What changes is KEDNY proposing to the recovery mechanisms for the 
15 
CSC Program to address the variability in CSC forecasting and 
16 
reimbursements?   
17 
A. 
In light of the increasing volatility and scope of unplanned CSC projects, such 
18 
as the Flatlands and SE865 projects, the Company is proposing to change the 
19 
existing CSC tracker mechanism to a full reconciliation of the difference 
20 
between forecast and actual spending (from the current 90 percent deferral 
21 


--- Page 50 ---
 
 
Page 48 of 120 
mechanism).  The Company is not proposing any other changes to the 
1 
mechanism or reporting requirements.  As set forth in the Joint Proposal 
2 
adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the CSC capital 
3 
reconciliation will work in conjunction with the net utility plant tracker.  A 
4 
demonstration of the CSC capital tracker mechanism is provided in the Revenue 
5 
Requirements Panel’s Exhibit __ (RRP-9), Schedule 2.      
6 
 
7 
Moreover, to address the similar variability in O&M spending demonstrated in 
8 
Exhibit __ (GIOP- 6), the Company is proposing to add an O&M tracker that 
9 
would fully reconcile the difference between forecast and actual spending in the 
10 
non-reimbursable “Support & Protect” O&M expense category.  This tracker 
11 
will provide assurance that the Company’s customers are not overcharged to 
12 
the extent expenses are lower than expected in any given year.  
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the status of the LaGuardia Airport Redevelopment 
15 
Project and the other significant CSC projects that are included in the 
16

A. 
The Port Authority of New York and New Jersey’s LaGuardia Airport upgrade

--- Page 52 ---
 
 
Page 50 of 120 
v. 
MRI 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Metropolitan Reliability Infrastructure (“MRI”) 
2 
Project. 
3 
A. 
The MRI project was included in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases as 
4 
a major gas system reliability project to provide increased supply diversity, 
5 
pressure support, outage contingency, operational flexibility, and operational 
6 
autonomy.  The project will maximize existing capacity and also will enable 
7 
proposed upstream capacity (e.g., from Williams’ NESE Project) that will 
8 
better position the Company to meet customers’ long-term supply needs over 
9 
the next 15 to 20 years.  Specifically, the MRI project will provide an 
10 
operational loop to the Brooklyn backbone system that will: 
11 
• Immediately improve reliability by increasing supply sources.  Sources 
12 
from any directly connected supply point will be accessible and gas may 
13 
be moved to any point across the Company’s system.  This will 
14 
significantly reduce the reliance on any individual gate deliveries, and 
15 
specifically dependence on gas from Consolidated Edison Company of 
16 
New York (“Con Edison”) at Newtown Creek. 
17 
• Allow the Brooklyn backbone system to be taken out of service for 
18 
system integrity work, while still maintaining gas operations. 
19 
• Enhance the effectiveness of remote control valves on the transmission 
20 
system by allowing increased utilization of remote valves in the event 
21 


--- Page 53 ---
 
 
Page 51 of 120 
of a system disturbance that requires a remote shut down (without 
1 
disrupting service to potentially thousands of customers). 
2 
• Provide for the movement of incremental supplies from both the 
3 
Narrows and the Lower New York Bay Lateral to any point across the 
4 
system, including transfer points with Con Edison.    
5 
 
6 
The MRI Project will enable KEDNY to move an additional 850 dekatherms 
7 
each day by 2021.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What is the current status of the MRI Project, and what is required to 
10 
complete the project? 
11 
A. 
The MRI Project is on target to complete the first two phases by the end of CY 
12 
2019.  The main portion of the project, phases one through four, involves 
13 
installation of approximately 33,100 feet of transmission main and a pig 
14 
launcher/receiver and a significant sewer crossing at Linden Boulevard.  Phases 
15 
one and two are nearing completion with approximately 20,000 feet of main 
16 
installed to date.  Phases three and four are pending construction and are 
17 
expected to be in service before the 2020-2021 winter heating season.  The 
18 
remaining work on the project, phase five, entails installation of approximately 
19 
7,000 feet of transmission main and a permanent pig launcher/receiver on the 
20 
Greenpoint facility.  Phase five is expected to be in service before the 2021-
21 


--- Page 54 ---
 
 
Page 52 of 120 
2022 winter heating season.  The forecast to complete the MRI project is 
1 
approximately $63.9 million in FY 2020, $100.0 million in the Rate Year and 
2 
$25.6 million in Data Year 1.  There is no projected spending for this project in 
3 
Data Years 2 and 3.   
4 
 
5 
vi. 
Renewable Natural Gas (“RNG”) Interconnections Program  
6 
Q. 
Please describe KEDNY’s new RNG Interconnections Program. 
7 
A. 
This is an innovative program that is part of the Company’s overall strategy to 
8 
encourage interconnection of third-party RNG projects to KEDNY’s natural 
9 
gas distribution system. High interconnection costs have proven to be a 
10 
significant impediment to the development and interconnection of new RNG 
11 
projects in KEDNY’s service territory.  Through this program, the Company 
12 
will install and maintain some of the necessary interconnection equipment, 
13 
specifically meters, analyzers, and odorization equipment, that currently is the 
14 
sole financial and operational responsibility of project developers, thus 
15 
lowering interconnection costs.  This program complements the Company’s 
16 
development of a standard RNG project interconnection agreement that resulted 
17 
from the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, as well as the Company’s 
18 
proposals toward decarbonizing the gas network (e.g., a new green gas tariff 
19 
offering), as discussed in the Future of Heat Panel’s testimony.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 55 ---
 
 
Page 53 of 120 
Q. 
What is the basis for the scope and forecast for the RNG Interconnections 
1 
Program?  
2 
A. 
Based on the number of inquiries KEDNY has received from RNG project 
3 
developers over the past several years, and RNG projects currently under 
4 
development, the Company forecasts approximately two RNG projects per year 
5 
are likely to mature to the interconnection phase during the Rate Year and Data 
6 
Years.  The capital forecast for this program of $0.9 million per year based on 
7 
the cost of meters, analyzers, and otherization equipment for two projects per 
8 
year.  A detailed description of the project and project costs is provided in the 
9 
data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).     
10

Q. 
How were the projected capital estimates derived? 
10 
A. 
In accordance with the Company’s budgeting policies and procedures, capital 
11 
budgets are prepared annually with a five year forward look.  Budget 
12 
projections are based on historical work levels and unit cost performance for 
13

ongoing mandated and routine work and programs, plus any identified new 
1

3

4

Q. 
What types of activities are associated with cost of removal? 
7 
The Company defines cost of removal as any work on an asset that results in it 
8 
being removed from the asset inventory, whether or not a different asset is 
9 
added in its place.  This type of work would include, but is not limited to, the 
10 
activities associated with disconnection, removal and disposal (or retirement in 
11 
place) of gas mains, gas services, and related facilities.

Q. 
Please describe some of the technologies and practices the Company uses 
6 
to reduce the total cost of its capital expenditures. 
7 
A. 
As discussed in the testimony of Company Witness Christopher J. Connolly, 
8 
National Grid has undertaken a comprehensive effort to evaluate the technology 
9 
and business processes that support its gas businesses (referred to as the Gas 
10 
Business Enablement (“GBE”) Program).  Among the core investments of the 
11 
GBE Program are new asset and work management, scheduling, geographic 
12 
information system, and field mobility solutions designed to improve the 
13 
Companies’ gas safety and operational performance.  While GBE is in process, 
14 
the Company continues to employ a number of technologies and best practices 
15 
designed to increase the efficiency and reduce the cost of its capital 
16 
expenditures.  These practices include: 
17 
• Increasing the amount of planned capital work (versus reactive work). 
18 
• Increasing coordination among capital programs to increase efficiencies 
19 
(e.g., leveraging LPP opportunities).  
20 


--- Page 60 ---
 
 
Page 58 of 120 
• Installing more small-diameter, high-pressure facilities that can be 
1 
installed at lower cost. 
2 
• Using smaller excavating equipment, increasing operating efficiency 
3 
and reducing instances of damage (because of decreased size and weight 
4 
of equipment). 
5 
• Using “coring and keyhole” technology to repair existing mains.  
6 
• Enhancing contractor management. 
7 
• On-site reporting for work crews in many large construction projects.  
8 
• Continuing to deploy CISBOT and CIP lining (as described in more 
9 
detail below). 
10 
In addition, as noted above, the Company’s Capital Delivery organization is in 
11 
the beginning stages of identifying opportunities for efficiencies that are 
12 
expected to reduce costs. 
13 
 
14 
A. Capital Planning, Budgeting, and Sanctioning Process 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the annual development of the Company’s capital plan. 
16 
Each year, the Company develops a ten-year capital plan to achieve its 
17 
performance objectives of delivering safe and reliable service.  Investment 
18 
Planning compiles proposed spending for programs and individual capital 
19 
projects.  Programs and projects are categorized into one of four spending 
20 


--- Page 61 ---
 
 
Page 59 of 120 
rationales (Mandated, Customer Connections, Reliability, and Non-
1 
Infrastructure).   
2 
 
3 
The Company currently uses a tool, C55 (known internally as “Copperleaf”), 
4 
for the prioritization and optimization of capital investments through a risk 
5 
scoring and prioritization methodology.  This tool uses criteria aligned to a 
6 
common scale to compare dissimilar inputs (e.g., financial and non-financial 
7 
inputs) to determine the set of investments, alternatives, and the timing of 
8 
projects that have the highest potential value.  
9 
 
10 
After the Companies rank the investments in C55, meetings are scheduled with 
11 
budget sponsors to review value models, project rankings, and various scenarios 
12 
produced through the optimization of investments to meet capital planning 
13 
targets.  Once plan targets have been approved by the Gas Business Unit, they 
14 
are submitted to Finance for review. 
15 
 
16 
In late fall, the capital plan is reviewed by the New York Jurisdictional 
17 
President (Company Witness John Bruckner) and the Vice President, Finance, 
18 
New York (Company Witness David Doxsee).  The New York Jurisdictional 
19 
President reviews the overall customer, service quality, and financial impacts 
20 


--- Page 62 ---
 
 
Page 60 of 120 
of the investment plan and may request changes to the level or mix of 
1 
investments.   
2 
 
3 
In early winter, the capital plan is presented to the National Grid USA Board of 
4 
Directors and the National Grid plc Executive Committee and, in early spring, 
5 
the capital portfolio is presented to the National Grid plc Board of Directors for 
6 
review and approval.   
7 
 
8 
Q.     Are there additional approvals needed before a project in the annual 
9 
capital plan may proceed? 
10 
A.     Yes.  Aside from the capital planning and budgeting process, specific delegation 
11 
of authority (“DOA”) approval must be obtained for any project in the ten-year 
12 
capital plan to proceed.  This process includes the sanctioning documentation 
13 
and review for projects over $1 million and other levels of review for smaller 
14 
projects.  Presently, all projects greater than $1 million require some level of 
15 
sanctioning documentation and review.  The U.S. Sanctioning Committee 
16 
(“USSC”) was established by the National Grid USA Board of Directors 
17 
specifically for this purpose.  Projects between $8 million and $25 million are 
18 
reviewed and approved by the USSC.  Projects between $25 million to $203 
19 
million are reviewed by the USSC and then are forwarded to a Senior Executive 
20 
Sanctioning Committee (“SESC”) for review and approval.  For projects 
21 


--- Page 63 ---
 
 
Page 61 of 120 
between $1 million and $8 million, the USSC has delegated review to an 
1 
informal committee led by the Investment Planning group and including, but 
2 
not limited to, managers and directors from the Regulatory, Estimating, Asset 
3 
Management, and Procurement groups.  The committee reviews and finalizes 
4 
sanctioning papers for these projects at a weekly meeting, and the committee 
5 
then forwards the final sanction documents to the executive sponsor of the 
6 
project for approval and signature.  Projects less than $1 million do not require 
7 
sanctioning and are approved through a supervisory delegation of authority 
8 
hierarchy based on certain established thresholds.   
9 
 
10 
Q.    
Please explain the difference between the DOA review and approval 
11 
(sanctioning) process and the approved five-year capital plan used to 
12 
forecast the Rate Year and Data Years. 
13 
A.      The timing of the sanctioning process is not aligned with the capital planning 
14 
process used to forecast the Rate Year and Data Years for purposes of the rate 
15 
filing.  As described above, the Company develops a long-term investment plan 
16 
that is used as the basis for the forecast for the Rate Year and Data Year 
17 
proposals.  Project sanctioning, however, normally occurs immediately prior to 
18 
the FY for which the investment is planned.  For example, the FY 2020 capital 
19 
plan will be formally sanctioned in early 2019.  Thus, the Company’s currently-
20 


--- Page 64 ---
 
 
Page 62 of 120 
sanctioned or partially-sanctioned projects do not yet represent the full capital 
1 
forecast proposed in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company’s DIMP impacts its capital investment 
4 
planning. 
5 
A. 
The DIMP involves a risk-based assessment of the Company’s distribution 
6 
system to identify threats in seven categories: corrosion, natural forces, 
7 
excavation damage, other outside force damage, material and weld failure, 
8 
equipment failure/malfunction and inappropriate operation.  The DIMP 
9 
requires evaluation and prioritization of the risks that these threats pose, and the 
10 
implementation of measures to address the highest risks with an emphasis on 
11 
leak management, enhanced damage prevention, operator qualification to 
12 
reduce human error and system replacement.  Consistent with the DIMP, the 
13 
Company prioritizes asset replacements in its investment plan based on a risk 
14 
ranking that considers, among other things, leak repair history, types of leak, 
15 
pipe material, surrounding geography, segment length, nearby construction 
16 
activity, field conditions, customer issues, open leaks and engineering 
17 
judgment.  The risk ranking factors are carefully designed to consider known 
18 
differences in the performance of asset subclasses, extensive experience with 
19 
asset failures, current performance data for the asset subclasses for various 
20 


--- Page 65 ---
 
 
Page 63 of 120 
threat categories, and subject matter experts’ analysis and opinions on the future 
1 
performance of the assets. 
2 
 
3 
B. Customer Connections Category of Spending 
4 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Customer Connections spending 
5 
category.  
6 
A. 
The Customer Connections category of work accounts for approximately eight 
7 
percent ($80.6 million) of the total planned capital investment in the Rate Year.   
8 
 
9 
KEDNY is required by the Public Service Law, the Commission’s regulations, 
10 
and tariff to extend service to new customers in its service territory in proximity 
11 
to the Company’s facilities, and to install new distribution main, up to a 100-
12 
foot tariff allowance per customer, to do so.  Expanding the availability of 
13 
natural gas in KEDNY’s service territory can bring significant economic 
14 
benefits in the form of energy cost savings for customers, job creation, and 
15 
increased local tax revenues.  Moreover, there are environmental benefits 
16 
associated with conversion to natural gas from other, higher emission fuels such 
17 
as fuel oil.  To enable new customer connections, the Company must make 
18 
significant capital investments in mains, services, and system reinforcements.  
19 
Customer Connection programs are designed to support forecast customer 
20 
demand and add new load by increasing system utilization in a cost-effective 
21 


--- Page 66 ---
 
 
Page 64 of 120 
way.  Customer Connection programs involve the installation of new mains, 
1 
services, and meters and include system reinforcement.   
2 
 
3 
The Customer Connections – Install Main, Install Services, Meter Purchases, 
4 
Install Meter, and Automatic Meter Reading (“AMR”) Programs and costs are 
5 
presented in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).   
6 
 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe recent customer demand trends in the Company’s service 
8 
territory. 
9 
A. 
The updated customer demand forecast shows steady demand in the 
10 
multifamily and commercial conversion market, steady growth in the multi-
11 
family new construction market, and an increase in the commercial new 
12 
construction market.  In the residential sector, the forecast shows an expected 
13 
increase in demand in residential new construction, but a dip in residential 
14 
conversions due to saturation in the market.  The completion of reliability 
15 
projects, including the Northern Queens Expansion Program and Brooklyn 
16 
Navy Yard projects, will enable new connections and are expected to increase 
17 
demand in previously under-served areas, subject to approval of the NESE 
18 
project, as discussed below.   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 67 ---
 
 
Page 65 of 120 
Q. 
If demand for new customer connections is expected to remain relatively 
1 
flat, what explains the increases in the Customer Connections forecasts? 
2 
A. 
The increase is driven by higher unit costs for main and service installation due 
3 
the factors discussed in Section III, above.  While units are not expected to 
4 
increase dramatically, the cost of main installation and reinforcements to serve 
5 
new customers increased as compared to prior years.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the Customer Connections – AMR Program. 
8 
A. 
The AMR Program includes the purchase and installation of AMR equipment 
9 
for new customer connection installations.  AMR equipment is not included in 
10 
the Company’s Customer Connections Purchase Meters budget.  Based on the 
11 
Company’s forecast, the Company will need to install between 9,000 and 
12 
10,000 AMRs per year during the Rate Year and Data Years at costs of 
13 
approximately $1.1 million each year.   
14 
  
15 
Q. 
Please describe the Gas System Reinforcement Program in the Customer 
16 
Connections category of spending. 
17 
A. 
The Gas System Reinforcements Program contains projects intended to ensure 
18 
that minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network 
19 
during periods of peak demand.  The Company models peak demand based on 
20 
the sendout forecasts developed by Analytics, Modeling and Forecasting 
21 


--- Page 68 ---
 
 
Page 66 of 120 
(Company Witness Theodore Poe).  As a result of demand for gas usage in its 
1 
service territory, KEDNY has determined that it is necessary to complete a 
2 
number of projects to ensure its ability to meet peak requirements.  These 
3 
reinforcement projects are essential to ensure the Company can continue to 
4 
serve growing gas usage load.  Without such reinforcements, the Company 
5 
estimates that more than 42,000 existing customers could experience loss of 
6 
service.    
7 
 
8 
KEDNY experienced new records for sendouts during the last two winter 
9 
heating seasons.  During the winter of 2017/2018, KEDNY recorded four of its 
10 
top ten sendout records, including a firm load record of 1,417,131, dekatherms 
11 
on January 6, 2018, when the average temperature was eight degrees Fahrenheit.  
12 
During the winter of 2018/2019, KEDNY recorded two of its top ten sendout 
13 
records, including a second highest firm load of 1387,550 dekatherms on 
14 
January 21, 2019 when the average temperature was thirteen degrees Fahrenheit.   
15 
 
16 
As is discussed in Section VII, below, and in the testimony of Company Witness 
17 
Elizabeth A. Arangio, additional capacity expected from Williams’ NESE 
18 
project is critical to meeting this level of increasing demand.  Moreover, the 
19 
recent growth in peak sendout underscores the need to ensure that the 
20 


--- Page 69 ---
 
 
Page 67 of 120 
Company’s gas distribution facilities are capable of maintaining minimum 
1 
system design pressures during periods of peak demand.     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please provide examples of System Reinforcement projects. 
4 
A. 
Examples of System Reinforcement projects include: 
5 
• Replacing undersized mains with larger diameter mains.  LPP is 
6 
targeted whenever practical during this work. 
7 
• Looping or connecting system endpoints by installing new main. 
8 
• System pressure uprates (e.g., 15 pounds per square inch (“psi”) to 60 
9 
psi). 
10 
• Installing new district regulators and replacing existing undersized 
11 
district regulators. 
12 
• Transferring existing low-pressure customers to an adjacent high-
13 
pressure main (i.e., load shedding). 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What explains the fluctuation in the Gas System Reinforcement forecast 
16 
from year-to-year?  
17 
A. 
The workplan for the Gas Systems Reinforcements Program is based on where 
18 
reinforcements are needed and, thus, is zero-based each year.  Estimates are 
19 
derived based on the types of projects needed, creating uneven spending year-
20 
to-year.   
21 


--- Page 70 ---
 
 
Page 68 of 120 
Q. 
Please describe the Kew Gardens Gate and Belmont Gate Station projects 
1 
in the Customer Connections category of spending. 
2 
A. 
These are major reinforcement projects that are separately budgeted and are 
3 
project managed by the Capital Delivery organization because they are complex 
4 
construction projects.  The Kew Gardens Gate Station will install a new 
5 
regulator station in Kew Gardens, Queens to address system pressure issues and 
6 
reduce dependency on deliveries from Con Edison’s transmission main.  The 
7 
project began in CY 2018 and is scheduled to be completed during the Rate 
8 
Year and includes a total forecast of approximately $22.7 million.  The Belmont 
9 
Gate Station Project will rebuild an existing station that is undersized to 
10 
increase capacity and address integrity issues.  The total cost of this project in 
11 
the Rate Years and Data Years is approximately $26.4 million, but there are no 
12 
costs included in the Rate Year forecast because the project is scheduled to 
13 
begin in Data Year 1 with the bulk of spending occurring in Data Year 3.   
14

Q. 
Is the Company proposing to continue its CISBOT and Large Diameter 
11 
Main Rehabilitation Programs for joint sealing and cast iron lining? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Company’s capital plan includes continuation of two programs, 
13 
included in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, that utilize a joint sealing 
14 
robot or (“CISBOT”) and cured-in-place pipe lining (“CIP”) to cost effectively 
15 
improve the safety and reliability of its large diameter mains.  These 
16 
technologies are proven to extend the life of the main, improve system 
17 
performance, and reduce future joint leaks on treated pipeline segments for 
18 
years.  As agreed in the Joint Proposal in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
19 
Cases, the Company will continue to include main segments reconditioned 
20 
through CISBOT and CIP lining in the inventory of LPP for eventual 
21 


--- Page 72 ---
 
 
Page 70 of 120 
retirement.  The Company intends to address approximately two and a half 
1 
miles of large diameter cast iron main in the Rate Year and each of the Data 
2 
Years at a cost of approximately $5 million each year, adjusted for inflation.  
3 
The Large Diameter Main Rehabilitation Program will address two miles in the 
4 
Rate Year and each of the Data Years using CIP lining at a cost of 
5 
approximately $14 million per year, adjusted for inflation.     
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing to line fewer miles each year at a higher 
8 
unit cost than reflected in the current rate plan? 
9 
A. 
Issues arose in the execution of CIP lining program over the past three years 
10 
where an entire segment of main could not be continuously lined due to 
11 
configuration issues.  In these instances, work had to be stopped and small 
12 
sections of main had to be replaced between segments that could be lined.  As 
13 
a lesson learned, the Company has adjusted it strategy to plan for replacement 
14 
of small segments that cannot be lined.  The proposed plan to line two miles is 
15 
more realistic and includes the time and funding to replace segments that cannot 
16 
be lined.      
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please explain KEDNY’s Cross Bore Remediation, Latent Damages, and 
19 
Atmospheric Corrosion Inside Inspections Programs. 
20 


--- Page 73 ---
 
 
Page 71 of 120 
A. 
The Cross Bore Remediation and Latent Damages Programs are existing 
1 
programs involving safety enhancements undertaken when the Company is 
2 
performing other mandated work, such as main replacement.  In the 2016 
3 
KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Company included a program to perform 
4 
inspections on older installations for cross bores, instances when plastic main 
5 
inadvertently bores through a sewer lateral as a result of horizontal directional 
6 
drilling.  The Company will complete inspections of historic HDD installations 
7 
by the end of CY 2020.  Going forward, the costs to inspect for cross bores on 
8 
new installations is embedded in main installation unit costs; however, a small 
9 
budget is necessary to mitigate cross bores that may be revealed through routine 
10 
inspections.  The capital investment plan includes $0.2 million in the Rate Year 
11 
and each of the Data Years, adjusted for inflation to remediate cross bores.  The 
12 
Latent Damages Program is a capital program to remediate third party damages 
13 
discovered during the course of normal operations.  The Company seeks to 
14 
continue this program at a funding level based on historic damage rates.  The 
15 
capital investment plan includes $0.4 million in the Rate Year and each of the 
16 
Data Years, adjusted for inflation.  
17 
 
18 
 
The Atmospheric Corrosion Inside Inspections Program is a similar proposed

--- Page 74 ---

Inside Service Line Inspections Order in Case 15-G-0244, baseline inspections 
1

2

3

4 
capital funding of approximately $0.7 million in the Rate Year (to address a 
5

6 
and $0.1 million per year in each of the Data Years, adjusted for inflation.

Program.

20

21 


--- Page 76 ---
 
 
Page 74 of 120 
warranted based on the Company’s engineering risk assessment that suggested 
1

2

3

4

5

6 
The current inventory is approximately 7,500 services, and KEDNY proposes

Year and each of the Data Years, adjusted for inflation.

A. 
Local Law 30 is a New York City Building Code that requires every gas service 
14 
line in the City to have a gas service valve or other emergency shut-off device 
15

16 
incurred tens of millions of dollars above the current rate plan allowance to 
17 
achieve significant completion of this work.  The program is now in its final 
18 
stage and will be complete by the end of the Rate Year.  Because this program 
19 
is ramping down in FY 2021, the O&M required for this program in the Rate 
20 
Year is significantly lower ($16.3 million) as compared to the Historic Test 
21 


--- Page 77 ---
 
 
Page 75 of 120 
Year ($63.9 million), and the capital forecast decreases to approximately $11.4 
1 
million dollars in the Rate Year.  There are no capital or O&M requirements for 
2 
this program in the Data Years.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Corrosion Control Program. 
5 
A. 
This program funds work consisting of field testing, monitoring, upgrades, and 
6 
repairs to existing corrosion control systems as mandated by Federal and State 
7 
Code requirements.  Part of this program addresses above ground gas mains at 
8 
bridge locations, which includes complete recoating of existing aged, dis-
9 
bonded, deteriorated or uncoated gas mains, as well as retirement of LPP where 
10 
it extends underground near these crossings.  In addition, this program 
11 
addresses the installation and testing of cathodic protection systems on buried 
12 
piping.  The Company’s forecast for the Corrosion Control is based on the 
13 
Historic Test Year program unit cost applied to a three-year historic average of 
14 
units (CY 2016 – CY 2018), adjusted for inflation in each year.  The Historic 
15 
Test year is more indicative of future costs because it was the first full year that 
16 
includes New York City’s new restoration and paving requirements.  The 
17 
capital investment plan includes approximately $1.0 million in the Rate Year 
18 
and each of the Data Years. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 78 ---
 
 
Page 76 of 120 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Valve Installation and Replacement 
1 
Program. 
2 
A. 
Federal and state regulations require installation, inspection, operation, and 
3 
maintenance of critical pipeline valves on all gas distribution systems.  The 
4 
purpose of these valves is to facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution piping 
5 
during gas emergencies such as third-party damage or water intrusion.  A 
6 
secondary purpose of these valves is to facilitate maintenance and pipe 
7 
replacement on associated distribution piping.   
8 
 
9 
This program will strengthen the Company’s emergency response capabilities 
10 
by improving the level at which Field Operations personnel can safely and 
11 
efficiently isolate sections of the distribution system while mitigating customer 
12 
impacts (e.g., reducing the duration of future outages).  Ensuring all critical 
13 
valves are properly maintained and operable is a key public safety function and 
14 
is essential to the effective operation of the Company’s gas distribution system.  
15 
The Rate Year forecast of approximately $0.1 million each year, adjusted for 
16 
inflation in each of the Data Years. 
17 
 
 
18 


--- Page 79 ---
 
 
Page 77 of 120 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Meter Changes Program. 
1 
A. 
The Meter Changes program involves the labor to replace gas meters that are 
2 
retired from service due to required periodic testing, damage, failure, or any 
3 
other reason.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Purchase Meters Program. 
6 
A. 
This program includes the purchase, testing, processing and delivery of gas 
7 
meters and associated instrumentation needed to support the Meter Change 
8

9 
operations. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Why is there an increase in costs for the Purchase Meters Program 
12 
beginning in FY 2020?  
13 
A. 
The Company has become aware that a certain population of meters installed 
14 
prior to 2003 contain cadmium, which can cause problems for testing lab 
15 
equipment.  Therefore, the Company is no longer refurbishing any pre-2003 
16 
meters.  This has increased the number of meters that may need to be replaced 
17 
each year going forward.

21 


--- Page 82 ---
 
 
Page 80 of 120 
local distribution companies; thus, KEDNY’s forecast for this project 
1 
represents KEDNY’s allocation of the total cost.     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Heater Installation and Pressure 
4 
Regulating Facilities Programs. 
5 
A. 
There are 40 natural gas heaters currently operating on the Company’s system.  
6 
Because high-pressure gas cools when reduced to a lower pressure, heaters are 
7 
required at pressure regulating stations to prevent freeze-ups that can impact 
8 
flow control devices.  In addition, cold gas temperatures can lead to reduced 
9 
pipe toughness and increased potential for frost heave and cold temperature-
10 
induced stresses.  The heater program adds new heaters (where required) and 
11 
replaces existing heaters that have reached the end of their useful lives.  The 
12 
Pressure Regulating Facilities category provides funding for replacement 
13 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control stations.   
14 
 
15 
The Pressure Regulating Facilities Program provides funding for replacement 
16 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control stations.  
17 
Pressure regulating facilities (or stations) are designed to control system 
18 
pressures and maintain continuity of supply during normal operating conditions 
19 
and during periods of peak gas demand.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 83 ---
 
 
Page 81 of 120 
KEDNY has assessed regulating stations on its system, evaluating factors such 
1 
as pressure, location, including whether the station is located in a FEMA 100 
2 
year or 500-year flood zone, and the number of dependent customers for each 
3 
station.  In addition, the assessment considered station condition, including pipe 
4 
corrosion, location and type of overpressure protection, automation, condition 
5 
of vaults, vault covers, wall sleeves, piping vents and ladders.  The results of 
6 
the assessment were used to create an overall risk rating for each station that 
7 
serves as the basis for prioritizing projects in this program.  The program 
8 
includes full or partial replacement of existing stations, and storm hardening 
9 
upgrades where necessary. 
10 
 
11 
The investments in the Pressure Regulating Facilities Program do not cover the 
12 
special project capital improvements to specific stations that are separately set 
13 
forth in the investment plan and described below. 
14

17

18 
included in any other blanket reliability programs.  These projects are described 
19 
in detail in the “Pressure Regulation Special Projects” data sheet included in 
20 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The projects target facilities that have the highest 
21 


--- Page 84 ---
 
 
Page 82 of 120 
potential customer impact.  Depending on the asset, these projects include 
1 
station replacement or rebuild, incorporation of odorization, gas quality 
2 
validation, pressure regulation, overpressure protection, storm hardening 
3
